Family History & Genealogy
Recovered family archive

The Neighbourhood

Black River Bridge, New Brunswick
Circa 1939

Donald E. M. Glendenning

294saved sections
149ancestor sections
2009original publication

Readable reconstruction from the September 2015 backup. The original prose is recovered wording in a new layout. Household reports and ancestor tables are rebuilt from decoded records, in the saved section order. This is not a facsimile of the printed book.

The saved database includes later descendants. One household report has an unresolved starting record, and two spouse records are unavailable in several reports. These gaps are marked. All 50 source images have now been recovered and incorporated into related sections. Surviving captions and saved person/family links guide their placement. The photo layout is reconstructed; a saved family association does not identify every person in a group photograph.

Ancestor tables use four generations, as specified in the saved charts. Household lists follow recovered preferred parent-child links and include spouses. Additional unverified family links are retained separately in the technical recovery data. Dates retain their recorded precision; an undecoded qualifier is stated explicitly.

Recovered October 2026. The original backup is preserved unchanged.

Section 1 of 294

The Neighbourhood (import message)

Recovered software import notice

This book was imported from an older Family Tree Maker file. There may be reports and charts that are missing. Because of differences between the old and new formats, book items may not include the same formatting or in some cases, the same data. The book import is intended to give you a template from which to recreate your pre-existing book.

This text item was added during import and should be deleted because it was not part of the book.

Title: The Neighbourhood

About the reconstructed edition (October 2026)

This edition was recreated in October 2026 from Donald E. M. Glendenning's saved Family Tree Maker project, dated September 2015, recovered from a backup of his laptop. His original written passages and the saved order of all 294 sections have been retained. The household and descendant reports were rebuilt from his recorded family information, and the ancestor charts are presented as four-generation tables.

All 50 photographs and other images used in this edition were recovered from the laptop backup and matched to the small previews saved in the project. Surviving captions and links to people or families guided their placement. The recovered images have not been altered; their positions on the page are part of the new layout.

This is a readable reconstruction, not an exact reproduction of the earlier printed book. The 2015 project may contain later additions, and the layout and pagination differ. Missing records and uncertain relationships are explicitly marked. Names, dates and family connections reflect the author's records and have not been independently verified. The original backup has been preserved unchanged.

Section 7 of 294

Introduction

Recovered original text

The Neighbourhood: Black River Bridge, New Brunswick Circa 1939

INTRODUCTION

This book grew out of a conversation with brothers, Norman and Kenneth in the old family home at Little Branch, New Brunswick in November 2003. We talked about earlier times and incidents at home and in the neighbourhood; later our minds and memories traveled the length of the Black River recalling who lived where. This book is the result of those conversations, my research, and information and photographs gathered during visits and conversations with family and friends from and in my former community. It is not a history but rather an inventory of households and community institutions.

My interest in genealogy had already resulted in a substantial body of information about people of the community and forms the basis of this document. In addition, as part of the Family Chronicle, an informal newsletter published since May, 1997, I found myself describing places and events of interest (www.glendenning.net/don).

My home in Black River overlooked both the Little Branch Bridge and the Little Branch School. That is where this inventory begins. From there the inventory continues, at least mentally, around the Branch, down to Hexham, across the Victoria Bridge and west along the north side of Black River past Route 11 into the Upper Settlement. Then it travels back to Little Branch via the south side of the Black River with a side trip into "Wellses Brook".

The first publication was a list of households in a Family Chronicle (No. 148, January 8, 2008); readers provided corrections. As the project progressed, however, and since much of the information was readily available in my family tree database, I decided to include a list of family members whether or not still living at home. Then I added an ancestor chart for heads of households - in those days I consider both parents to fit that category.

The name for a household is the name as I knew it; in a few cases that name did not match the name of the oldest person in the household, usually a parent, and this confused my computer so I've tried to compensate through the addition of an "Errata Sheet".

While I have shown, as best I can remember, all children of the household, many had moved way from home prior to 1939. Including birth and death dates is tricky, however, in the interest of making the document more useful for people interested in their family tree, I included both. Names and dates, for the most part, were gathered from a variety of sources including family, newspaper clippings, other genealogists, cemetery stones and church reports, etc; most, I hope and believe are correct but there were occasions where I simply had to make a choice between two or more conflicting dates.

To further increase the usefulness of the book, I have allowed space on each family page for readers to add their own information and recollections. Make this a living record of your community.

Readers, if there are any, may ask why spend scarce time on such a project. The simple answer is that I enjoyed the project. I should add, also that I have many happy memories of Black River and my youth, I feel fortunate to have grown up on a farm and experienced rural life. As evidence, my travels have taken me to many corners of the earth, over forty countries in all, with my place of birth on my passport shown as Little Branch, New Brunswick - I'm proud of that entry. It is also my hope that the information contained herein will be of interest and of value to readers with a particular interest in their roots.

Recalling who lived where seventy years ago is bound to include errors; other omissions are simply the result of lengthy senior's moments. If readers find omissions or errors, please let me know so that they can be corrected. Email me at don@glendenning.net

While I take full responsibility for the material contained herein, I would be remiss if I did not thank many people including:brothers Norm and Ken, Everard and Bertie MacLean, Richard MacDougall, Elbie and Shirley Donnelly, Francis and Phyllis MacNaughton, Della (Adams) O'Neill, George Watling, Jack Wilson, Harold MacLean, Ernest MacLean, Cameron MacDonald, Richard MacDougall, Donald Edge. My tendency not to record contributions when they were made may have resulted in some omissions. Even if your name is not included, I want you to know that I appreciate your help. I've also attributed photos as best I can remember the.

Again I thank my wife, Carol, for her patience in this, another of my many projects.

I hope that you enjoy reading this volume as much as I enjoyed creating it.

Don Glendenning Charlottetown, PEI

January, 2009

Section 8 of 294

Little Branch Bridge

Recovered original text

Little Branch Bridge circa 1954 by Glendenning

Little Branch Bridge

Route 117 East exited Black River through the Little Branch Bridge on its way to Escuminac. I say "through" rather than "over" because the main body of the bridge was covered.

The Bridge was made of two spans - one long and covered and the other short and open. The spans met at a common pier which was a wooden crib filled with field stone and clad with vertical plank. Sometime in the 1940's, as I recall, the truss was removed from the small span (see photo above) and another pier was placed in the middle.

A sign, mounted on the end of the bridge, read:

TWENTY DOLLARS FINE FOR DRIVING OVER THIS BRIDGE FASTER THAN A WALK

Other bridges may have had a similar sign but this is the only one I remember. The reason for the caution is the possibility that horses, while trotting, cold harmonize their action with the movement of the bridge,thereby putting extra strain on bridge members. The covered bridge has since been replaced with a concrete span.

On the southwest side of the bridge was a small wharf. Since transportation in the early years was by water, the wharf served as a point for picking up and dropping off goods and passengers. It was also used to tie up boats when logs were being rafted down the Little Branch River.

The Little Branch River, especially at the Bridge, was a popular spot for trout fishing with the favoured spot being on the wharf at the south west corner where the channel was close to the wharf. As the tide turned in the evening, was also considered a good time for fishing. The bridge, and Glendenning's shore nearby, were also favoured swimming spots

Back to book controls
Section 9 of 294

Little Branch School

Recovered original text

Undated photo of the Little Branch School (Courtesy Everard and Bertie MacLean)

Little Branch School

The Little Branch School sits at the junction of Route 117 and the Little Branch Road (formerly the MacKenzie Road). A. W. MacDougall in his book, Black River and its First Settlers, tells us that the first school was erected about 1850; it burned shortly thereafter but was soon replaced. The Little Branch School was in District 71/2 in the Parish of Glenelg Parish in Northumberland County. An out door toilet, with separate doors for boys and girls, can be seen perched over the cove just beyond the school.

The Little Branch Schools served children from along the Branch Road, Hexham to the end of the Victoria Bridge and along the south side of the Black River as far as the community hall. During the year 1933-34, it served 50 students with a few ending their schooling by Christmas and a few commencing their school starting in January; the teacher was Donald J. MacLean who held a Second class license.

In 1939, The Little Branch School became one of seven schools in the newly-created Miramichi Rural High School district providing instruction only to the end of Grade Six. Students in the higher grades travelled by bus to the newly constructed Miramichi Rural High School

Section 31 of 294

Fowlie's Mill

Recovered original text

Undated photo of Fowlie's Mill

Fowlie's Mill

Although there were a number of sawmills in the area, it appears that the first gristmill was located on the Little Branch River. LOOKING BACK: A History of Bay du Vin, New Brunswick provides the following quotation from the Gleaner in 1833:

"“Mr. George Fowlie erected a grist mill on the Little Black River, which commenced grinding last week. We have seen some bread made of the flour manufactured at this mill and it was of superior quality. It has a never failing stream of water and will be a great accommodation to the inhabitants of Bay du Vin and adjoining settlements where such an establishment was much wanted.” The Fowlie mill was burned and rebuilt twice. “

Eldon Fowlie is quoted in an undated clipping in my possession as saying the grist mill built in the early 1800's, was paddle powered and was built by his great grandfather. The mill was converted to turbine operation in the 1850's. At some point, a sawmill was added.

My recollection is that, by 1939, the water-powered saw mill was the largest employer in the community; the grist mil, however, was but a shell In fact, in the late 1930's, the old grist mill served as a training centre in carpentry and blacksmithing. It was part of a national program to provide some basic skill training to the many unemployed youth in the area. Participants boarded with local families; Hass Ross and Hugh Gibson boarded at my home. Shortly after, many of these young men were off to war.

Back to book controls

Section 65 of 294

Adams' Grocery Store

Recovered original text

The grocery store was entered through the shed (open door behind Elmer Adams) the grocery store was in the room with the small window to the left. Photo at right shows gas pump. Adams' Grocery Store

Today, we would call it a convenience store but then it was simply Adams' Grocery.

The Adams home was large, two-story home with an attached shed and, beyond that and still attached, a grocery store. One actually entered the store through the shed. Behind the store and also entered through the shed was the icehouse which was the store's refrigeration area.

The store was well stocked with shelves of canned goods, packaged goods, matches, cigarettes and pipe tobacco. He also carried meat but the only kind I can recall is canned meat and bologna.

One also could purchase oil lamps and globes, lantern globes, wicks, burners and, of course kerosene. The latter was stored in a barrel in the shed. One took ones own can or bottle to the store to be filled.

Across the yard from the store to your right as you drove in, was the gas pump. Gas pumps stood, I would think, about 10-12 feet tall but the gasoline itself was stored in a tank under ground. Gas was pumped by hand into the glass “bottle” at the top with the number of gallons marked on the glass.. One placed an order for gas by the gallon, Mr. Adams pumped the gas into the tank at the top judging the amount visually, then putting the hose into the gas tank filler, drained the gas from the pump into the car. It was gravity feed all the way.

Back to book controls

Section 72 of 294

Sinclair's Blacksmith Shop

Recovered original text

The Blacksmith Shop

Sinclair Williston was a blacksmith as well as a farmer; his shop was on the left side of his lane - a long one, leading to his house and barn. Like all blacksmith shops, had its peculiar sights, sounds and smell. As I recall, it wasn't a large shop and just outside the door was a “pen” to restrain unruly horses when they were being shod.

As I recall, the forge was made of stone with a huge bellows/pump to fan the flame; Sinclair worked the bellows by means of a short pole with one end fastened to the bellows and the other within easy reach as he stood at the forge. Beside the forge were the tools of the trade, the anvil was in front and the tub of water nearby.

Sinclair did all kinds of blacksmith work. He made shoes for sleds, made new links for chains, shortened the steel rims on wagon wheels, cut and welded (in the forge) braces and metal pieces of all kinds and, of course, shod horses. It was mostly because of the latter that I remember being there. If I couldn't do much else, I could take the team to the blacksmith shop and wait while the horses were being shod.

Back to book controls

Section 91 of 294

Victoria Bridge

Recovered original text

Victoria Bridge (From a painting by Isobel Jenkins courtesy Richard MacDougall)

Victoria Bridge

The Victoria Bridge spanned the Black River near its mouth; of the three covered bridges spanning the Black River, Victoria was the longest with lengthy causeways and two piers under the covered span. Ken Glendenning remembers stairs leading down to the water at the centre piers from which a person could get into a boat. The wharf at the right was a more recent addition.

In 1932, a tornado passed through Black River damaging many farm structures. It also moved the Victoria Bridge on its piers and knocked down some bridge timbers. Dad, John and the author, with a team of horses, were in the bridge when the tornado struck. The horses would not move and John took them by the bridle to lead them through the bridge.

On June 10, 1938, Larry Brideau and John Paul were drowned at Victoria Bridge. They were peeling pulp at Hexham and decided to go for a swim at the end of the day. John Paul drowned while trying to rescue his friend. Sometime, probably in the 1940's, a high wind and heavy storm surge did major damage to the bridge carrying some of the heavy beams up-river to McKnight's shore. Later the bridge burned and was not replaced.

Back to book controls

Section 108 of 294

Victoria School

Recovered original text

Undated photo of the Victoria School courtesy of Louise MacDougall

Victoria School

The Victoria School became one of seven schools in the newly-created Miramichi Rural High School district in 1939. When the MRHS opened, grades one to six continued at the each of the small schools; the new high school offered instruction in Grades seven through eleven

Section 141 of 294

Cameron Bridge

Recovered original text

Undated photo of Cameron Bridge courtesy of Francis MacNaughton

Cameron Bridge

The Cameron Bridge (or Black River Bridge to separate it from the Victoria and Little Branch bridges) provided a crossing for the road between Loggieville and Escuminac, the most widely used corridor of the day; the bridge and the adjacent school are so named because of the preponderance of settlers named Cameron in the immediate area. Like other covered bridges, each winter a nearby resident was designated to snow the bridge to ease the travel of sleds.

The bridge, the shortest of the three spanning the Black River, was badly damaged by fire in 1954 and destroyed by fire in 1970. Following the fire, a Bailey bridge provided temporary crossing. A new pre-cast concrete bridge was constructed in 1976.

This was, and probably still is, considered a great trout fishing location.

Back to book controls
Section 142 of 294

Cameron School

Recovered original text

Undated photo of Cameron School courtesy of Elbie and Shirley Donnelly

Cameron School

In 1825, residents petitioned successfully for a school which, as I understand it, was erected near the lane now leading to St. Stephen's Church. This school appears to have served all of Black River and as far east as Bay du Vin.

About 1845, the district was divided with one school being build at Middle Black River (above) and another at Bayside (now Miramichi). A few years later a school was erected at Little Branch; shortly thereafter, the Little Branch School burned but was soon replaced.

The school at Middle Black River, the Cameron School, was so named because most of the early settlers in the area carried the Cameron name..

The Cameron School became one of seven schools in the newly-created Miramichi Rural High School district in 1939. When the MRHS opened, grades one to six continued at the local school; the new high school offered instruction in Grades seven through eleven

John W's Lumber mill

Across the road from his home and back near the edge of the woods, John W. MacNaughton operated a sawmill. It was powered by either a gasoline or diesel engine.

Section 180 of 294

Upper Black River School

Recovered original text

Upper Black River School (Glendenning, 2007)

Upper Black River School

Although the date of construction of the Upper Black River School is not known, my mother, who was born in 1890, and her siblings walked to school from the Meadows. The school, an adjacent community hall and a nearby barn belonging to Robert MacNaughton, were completely destroyed by a tornado in 1903. Date of rebuilding not known.

Upper Black River did not become part of the Miramichi Rural High School district in 1939 although some students from there boarded down river and attended the new school.

Back to book controls

Section 252 of 294

St. Paul's Presbyterian Church

Recovered original text

Undated photo of St Paul's Presbyterian Church by Glendenning

St Paul's Presbyterian Church

Prior to 1836, residents of Black River traveled to Bay du Vin to attend church, although nominally Presbyterian, was used by other denominations as well. In 1836 a new church, St Stephen's Presbyterian, was opened in Black River and was situated within the bounds of what is now the Black River cemetery. The original church building was replaced in 1906.

Following Church Union in 1925, St. Stephens Presbyterian Church became St. Stephens United Church and continuing Presbyterians built a new Presbyterian Church, St. Paul's, shown in the photo above. St. Paul's Presbyterian Church was opened on July 21, 1930. During the early years St. Paul's was shared a summer minister with Knox Presbyterian Church in Kouchibouguac. Ministers stayed one week in each community traveling to the other each Sunday. Student ministers boarded with local families, including mine.

Section 262 of 294

The Manse

Recovered original text

Original St. Stephen's Church Manse

The United Church Manse

Although St. Stephens Presbyterian Church was built in 1836, the manse was not constructed until 1880. With church Union in 1925, the Church and the manse became the property of St. Stephens United church. In the mid 1900's, a new manse was constructed nearby and the original manse became the property of the Elbie and Shirley Donnelly.

Back to book controls
Section 263 of 294

St. Stephen's United Church

Recovered original text

Undated photo of St. Stephen's United Church by Glendenning

St. Stephen's United Church

Prior to 1836, residents of Black River traveled to Bay du Vin to attend church which, although nominally Presbyterian, was used by other denominations as well. By 1820, Gunn's Point, although privately owned, began to be used as a burial ground. It is not surprising; therefore, that this became the location in 1836 for a new church, St Stephen's Presbyterian. The original church was situated within the bounds of what is now the cemetery; a stone cairn near the point marks the location of the original church. The original building was replaced in 1906 by the one shown in the photo above.

Following Church Union in 1925, St. Stephens Presbyterian Church became St. Stephens United Church and continuing Presbyterians built a new Church, St. Paul's, a half-mile further west.

For a more detailed description, readers are referred to pages 31-56 of Black River and Its First Settlers by A. W. MacDougall, 1935. A recent short history of the church and the cemetery can be found in an article by Ernie MacLean in the Spring 2001 Issue of Generations.

Back to book controls
Section 264 of 294

Black River Community Cemetery

Recovered original text

Undated photo of Black River Cemetery by Glendenning

Black River Cemetery

The Black River Cemetery is situated on a point of land known as Gunn's Point, and later Church Point; it was part of Lot No. 4 originally granted to Enoch Gunn on June 3, 1812. The first burial, I believe, was that of Robert MacBeath in 1823. Although the nearest church was in Bay du Vin, other burials of Black River folk took place at Gunn's Point even though the land was privately owned. In 1836, a new church, St. Stephen's Presbyterian, was constructed at the point and purchased the old burial ground. more detailed history of the church and the cemetery can be found in an article by Ernie MacLean in the Spring 2001 Issue of Generations.

Following Church Union in 1925, St. Stephens United Church gave full burial rights to the Presbyterians and today, this sacred plot, is considered a community burial ground.

Section 267 of 294

Black River Community Hall

Recovered original text

The Black River hall in 1967 at which time the front entry was being added. (Courtesy of Everard and Bertie MacLean.)

Black River Community Hall

Construction of the first community hall in Black River commenced in the fall of 1930 and the first events were held in early 1931. The following year the hall burned and in 1933 the second Black River Community Hall was erected.

Both the first and second halls were essentially the same, two-story buildings 50 by 34 feet on concrete footing and clad with eastern cedar shingles. (the two story section behind the scaffolding constitutes the second community hall). The ground floor included a small stage with access from both sides; the interior was covered with, I believe, narrow pine or Douglas fir sheathing placed diagonally. On the left of the stage was a set of stairs leading to the second floor, which was unfinished with a small room directly above the stage. The hall was heated by a wood stove and oil lamps hung from the walls. An outdoor privy stood at the back of the lot.

Section 293 of 294

Miramichi Rural High School

Recovered original text

Undated photo of Miramichi Rural High School

Miramichi Rural High School

Although not located in Black River, the Miramichi Rural High School (MRHS) catchment area included Black Rive. MRHS was the first of a new series of rural high schools in New Brunswick; the second school was on Deer Island. Before that, the few students who engaged in high school studies did so in Loggieville or Chatham and usually stayed with relatives.

The school was officially opened in October, 1940, (I believe with 67 students) although classes moved into the school in January of that year, with A.T. Patstone as principal and Miss Beryl MacAloney and Miss Jean Barns as the first teachers. Officially, the school served the school districts of Black river Bridge, Little Branch, Victoria, Bayside, Bay du Vin river and Bay du Vin Beach. The school was built, as I recall for $13,000

Three years earlier, the Rev. Douglas Smith had provided private tutoring at the Rectory in Bay du Vin. Classes proved popular and two teachers, Elizabeth Stevenson and Mrs. Dorothy Clatonburg were added and classes were offered in the church basement and church hall. Students from Black river walked or cycled to school and traveled by horse and sled in the winter. Even after MRHS opened, I remember Norman taking a horse and sled to Bay Side picking up fellow students along the way and leaving the horse across the road from the school during the day in Harrison Smith's barn. Of course, one always carried some hay and oats for the horse while it was stabled; Norman always checked and watered the horse at noon.

My first visit to MRHS was late in the fall of 1939 when George Watling and biked down to see it. It was an impressive building for a rural area. As the photo above shows, it was two story with two classrooms on each level at the front and an auditorium on the second floor at the back. At the back on the lower floor were the principal's office, boys and girls washrooms and the furnace room.

MRHS had electric lights, 32 volt as I recall, operated from a bank of glass-encased batteries and had a small gasoline engine which switched on automatically to charge the batteries. It also had a coal-fired furnace. A very tall flagpole stood in the front yard by the driveway. It looked like a ship's mast. The pole, not the flag on the pole, had to be raised and lowered using a team of horses.

It soon became obvious that transportation was required if the youth of the community were to take full advantage of the facility, Consequently, for a while, transportation was provided by Goymer Williston on the back of his truck protected from the weather by a canvas top; Later a closed bus was operated by the Godfrey Brothers.

The school was the first rural high school in the Province; in addition to the usual academic subjects, students learned Homme economics and the boys had wood working and blacksmithing. Students maintained garden plots as part of their program and the produce was canned, stored and used by the Home Ec department The school was built, as I recall for $13,000 The school was built, as I recall for $13,000

Back to book controls
Section 294 of 294

About the Author

Recovered original text

ABOUT THE AUTHOR

Don Glendenning was born and raised on a farm on the banks of the Little Branch (Little Black) River in New Brunswick; he attended Little Branch School and St. Paul's Presbyterian Church, is a graduate of the Miramichi Rural High School and attended scout meetings, dances and other community events in the Black River community hall. While growing up, he worked on the family farm, loaded pulp and pit props on steamers along the Miramichi; his first teaching role was at Kouchibouguac, Kent County, New Brunswick but later taught at the Miramichi Rural High School, the first of a new breed of rural high schools built in New Brunswick just prior to WW2.

He has fond memories of Black River many of which are recorded in his Family Chronicle.an occasional electronic newsletter which can be found at www.glendenning.net/don

His formal education included studies at the Teachers' College in Fredericton, the New Brunswick Institute of Technology (now the New Brunswick Community College) in Moncton, the University of London (England) on a Lord Beaverbrook Overseas Scholarship, Bradley University in Peoria, Illinois and Indiana University in Bloomington, Indiana. Don taught at the elementary, secondary and post secondary level in New Brunswick, worked as a training specialist in Ottawa for a number of years before moving to Prince Edward Island where he became the Founding President of Holland College.

In 1958 Don married Carol Louise Chesney of Peoria, Illinois, a graduate of the Methodist School of Nursing. Their family includes a son, Brian, a Research Scientist with the NRAO and living in Socorro, New Mexico; Janet a policy analyst with the Canadian Transportation Agency in Ottawa; daughter-in-law, Marie, an IT specialist with National Radio Astronomy Observatory; granddaughter, Lisa (recently married to Kevin Tegtmeier) a graduate student at the University of Washington in Seattle; and granddaughter, Laura a recent graduate of the University of New Mexico and living at home.

Charlottetown, Prince Edward Island, Canada

January, 2009
